ParlaMint-DK_20170510131212	Here you go for reading.
ParlaMint-DK_20170510131215	The question is: please inform the Minister: what is the Ministry's approach to using a business approach in relation to hiring more people in SKAT?
ParlaMint-DK_20170510131228	Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170510131229	The view of the Ministry of Finance is that there is no concrete basis for creating a business case model. One might ask what the gain from a particular extra job in either the SKAT or somewhere else is concrete in relation to it. One might ask: What is the SKAT doing? SKAT does, of course, take a risk-based approach. This means that, in principle, the first employee of the SKAT has to deal with those cases where it is believed that there is the greatest likelihood of error, tax evasion or fraud, or that someone else is trying to avoid paying the tax that the law grants. The next employee must then take what is second most exposed etc. etc. This means that you cannot just make an average consideration of what the average revenue per year is. We have an employee today, and so say that you can get it forward-looking as well. Another approach could then be to ask what it is you get out of a concrete project where an employee is employed. However, the idea in the SKAT has been to change the legislation, to automatically report, so as to avoid problems. We can easily have a discussion about whether it has fulfilled the wishes we have had all the way through. But the approach has been that instead of checking your way out of mistakes, you had to create systems that made no mistakes. Thus, with increasingly automatic reporting, more and more Danes really need to change their tax information before SKAT can make an annual statement. It is possible to lock fields so that you can make an alert yourself, but if the report you are making is extraordinarily large or something, then SKAT must check it before it is approved. This has given rise to systems which make it smaller and smaller for ordinary employees, for example. may be misreported either because it is automatic or because their alerts, if they stick out specifically, are checked manually or systematically through the SKAT. Therefore, there are problems in making such a businesscase approach as the questioner asks.

It is true that the government has gradually decided to deploy the funds of the rural pool for research, and this is entirely in line with the government's general desire for more competition in the performance of public tasks. Therefore, from 2018 onwards, there will no longer be a fixed grant from the rural pool to the Centre for Rural Research. The aim of the competition exposure is to get as much quality research as possible for the money, because research, dissemination and new knowledge about rural areas are important elements in the work of creating a better balance for Denmark, and this is a very high priority for the government. The University of Southern Denmark has a long tradition of researching rural areas and will also be able to obtain support from the rural pool in the future on an equal footing with other actors in the field. It is clear that it will be a conversion for the Centre for Rural Research to go from a fixed annual allocation to competing with other players on the funds, and therefore support for the Centre will not fall abruptly, but will be phased out gradually. It is a general challenge to provide external funding for all actors active in rural research, but healthy competition can also be a driving force for better research, and the government therefore wants to give more players the opportunity to offer public research funding in rural areas. The CLF is not the only institution that is researching in this area, so are others, and therefore I do not believe that the gradual phasing-out of the flat-rate funding for the CLF removes focus from rural conditions and the development of national research. On the contrary, I actually expect that the restructuring of the pool's research funds will increase competition to create new knowledge and contribute to overall research in this area. The Centre's fixed grant has previously been awarded through a performance contract with the Danish Business Authority, and the funds have been earmarked for concrete projects in this contract. So it is not true that the Centre has received a basic grant, but support has been given to specific research projects which the Centre has offered from year to year, and it will also be the future of the Centre that we must bid and win the competition to have the best projects which will then be supported.

ParlaMint-DK_20170510143114	Here's the questioner for reading.
ParlaMint-DK_20170510143116	Thank you, sir. President. The first question is this: does the Minister not think that it is reasonable for a Dane who travels to the big world to work and establish a family with a wife and children to be allowed to settle in this country with his whole family?
ParlaMint-DK_20170510143132	The minister.
ParlaMint-DK_20170510143133	Thank you for the question. In recent times, there has been some mention of the possibility for foreign Danes to return to Denmark with their families. I cannot go into specific matters, but I would really like to explain the background to the cases. For some foreign dancers have found it harder to return home to Denmark with their foreign family, because the 26-year rule, which is an exception to the attachment requirement, no longer applies. It does not do so as a result of the judgment of the Court of Human Rights in the so-called Biao case. As a result of the judgment, we can no longer maintain the rule. I am very sorry that we have to lift the 26-year rule. I think it takes an important account of, among other things, the situation. The possibility for foreign Danes to return home to Denmark. However, once the Court of Human Rights has delivered a judgment as it is, we will of course have to correct Denmark, and I also know that the Social Democratic Party believes so. However, the government has tried to find a solution as soon as possible when the 26-year rule could no longer be applied. It's been hard, I don't want to hide it. Because we want a solution that does not, of course, break a big gap in the rules and thus opens a floodgate for massive immigration through family reunification. But on the other hand, we would like to give the foreign dancers the opportunity to return home with their families. The solution we have arrived at here in the short term is a residence scheme for accompanying families of foreign Danes with certain employment qualifications. If the Government's bill is adopted, foreign dancers who in Denmark are offered a position covered by the Trusted List or the Amount Scheme will be able to obtain their foreign family to Denmark under the same conditions as foreign workers. Some foreign dancers will therefore not be able to meet the normal family reunification conditions, including, for example: the attachment requirement, in order to get their families to Denmark. Instead, the conditions imposed on families of foreign workers will have to be met. will be required for the family to be self-supporting and to have a common residence. ParlaMint-DK_20170510151115	Here's the questioner.
ParlaMint-DK_20170510151117	Thank you for that, President. The question is: Does the Minister agree with the Deputy Chairman of Agriculture & Food, Lone Andersen, when she says in Information on 3 May 2017 that the sales [of agricultural properties] should be registered so that you can see who owns Danish agriculture, cf. Information article • Agriculture Chairman: Purchase of land by foreign investors may be slowed down?
ParlaMint-DK_20170510151142	The minister.
ParlaMint-DK_20170510151143	Thank you, President, and thank you for the question. The Danish Agriculture and Fisheries Authority states that the Danish Parliament, by amending the Agricultural Act in 2014, granted extended access for domestic and foreign companies to acquire agricultural properties. The aim was to remove barriers to agricultural access to finance. Thus, a foreign investor can buy a Danish agricultural property directly through a foreign company. A foreign investor can also purchase a Danish agricultural property indirectly via a Danish company or as a private person. The capital of the Danish company may finally be owned by a foreign parent company. Purchases must be reported to the Danish Agricultural and Fisheries Authority to ensure compliance with the agricultural law's residence requirements. There is no requirement to report in relation to the share capital of the company. The Agriculture and Fisheries Authority further states that there is a general increase in the acquisition of agricultural property in company form, which is consistent with the purpose of the amendment of the Agricultural Act. Personal acquisitions continue to constitute the major part, but the number of acquisitions is increasing. Overall, developments since 2013 have been as follows: In 2013 2,824 personal acquisitions and 361 corporate acquisitions were registered for a period of nine months and this figure developed in the future over the years. For me, it is important for agriculture to have access to finance, not whether this funding comes from Danish or foreign investors. The development is in line with the purpose of the 2014 amendment to the Agriculture Act and I see no immediate need to create a register specifically intended to provide information on, among other things. ownership of agricultural property by foreign investors in Denmark.

Health experience, which has inspired the pool, is that financial incentives work and help create a clear management focus. I have no immediate reason to believe that it would be different in the field of school. When we have chosen to translate the pool on the basis of the grades at the 9th class exams in Danish and mathematics, it is because test grades are crucial for students' further opportunities in education and employment. Test marks are therefore a solid guide to where the challenges are greatest. ParlaMint-DK_20170510154915	The minister.
ParlaMint-DK_20170510154916	Thank you for the question. The rules on driving and rest periods for drivers are laid down in the driving and rest periods regulation. On the other hand, the penalties for infringements of those rules are laid down in national law. Rules are, of course, there to be complied with and it can have a significant impact on road safety if a driver, for example. is tired and therefore unattractive in traffic. I therefore also believe that it is a problem if drivers do not comply with their driving and rest periods. With regard to the first possible solution proposed by the honourable Member, I would like to stress that the driving and rest periods regulation at home is interpreted as meaning that it is illegal to hold the regular weekly rest in the cab. The rule is, however, very difficult to enforce, as by its very nature it requires long-term observation of the vehicle. Following this, it can be pointed out that no legal action has been brought in the past on these infringements. If the sanction is to be tightened up, it should be ensured that it is proportionate to the nature of the infringement. It would not be proportionate to choose a level of sanctions which is significantly above what is common in the area of driving and rest periods. Last week, I have a question from Mr. Magnus Heunicke indicated that tightening the penalty to German level would be roughly equivalent to the penalty for the most serious violations of the tachograph regulation in the driving and rest period bill we are currently considering here in the Folketing. However, the imposition of a higher fine would not in itself alter the difficulties involved in enforcing the ban. The question is therefore how much effect will be. With regard to the construction of rest buildings on industrial sites, to which the questioner also refers as a possible solution, I have to say that it is a municipal matter to decide whether to authorise the behaviour of such buildings in commercial areas. This must, of course, be done in accordance with the local plan, building regulations and other relevant rules. ParlaMint-DK_20170510161855	This proposal, as has already been mentioned in the debate, is about changing the phasing-in of electric cars in particular to the general registration system. Therefore, the parties to the agreement, of which Dansk Folkeparti is not part, have chosen to postpone the phase-in by 2 years, so that it remains at 20% or until 5,000 cars have been sold. This is what we in the Danish People's Party think is an excellent model. It has always been written in the text of the agreement that if car sales were to be much larger or much less than expected, the parties to the agreement would have to meet again and find a new solution to it. That is what we are doing here, and that is quite reasonable. There are also a number of other small things in the bill, among others. that electric cars can charge the low rate for another 2 years, which is also excellent. Then there is one point that I am a little more critical of and would like to ask for in-depth answers to, possibly during the committee procedure, and this is the amendment of the basis for calculating green ownership tax, the compensatory and registration tax on gas-powered cars and gas-powered hybrid plugin vehicles, which is, of course, part of the green ownership tax and the countervailing charge. There we would like it to be calculated on the basis of energy efficiency, where it used to be based on CO2 and that is to achieve harmonisation with the other fuels, but the challenge is that it makes it significantly more expensive to have a gas car, although it is actually something that is quite positive for our climate. What is a little special is that it is said that the reason for this change is that there must be equality between technologies. That argument sounds a little hollow, because the whole legislative proposal is about making differences between the technologies, that is to say that you have a lower tax on electric cars than on ordinary petrol and diesel cars. So I do not think there is really any consistency in the argument when it is said that a gas car that is actually good for the climate should rise in tax due to equality, while, in general, the whole legislative proposal assumes that electric cars must be cheaper for a period of time. And that seems a little strange to me. Because you use it as an excuse that it is because of EU rules. But Denmark has never given up sovereignty in tax matters, so I am a little puzzled about why the EU should decide how we choose to adjust our taxes in this area. There is then a question which may be answered by the Minister when he comes to the floor later. It is about the funding in it, where it is obvious, of course, that there has been a bag of money, perhaps you cannot say that, but an excess revenue because you have not sold so many electric cars. I just want to know if the tax minister can say anything about whether or not the amount of money you spend here will go up. Otherwise, I would like to say that I think it is excellent that there is, among other things. More electric cars or gas cars or hydrogen cars are coming on the Danish roads to reduce our CO2 But I think it is generally a pity that we are making such special rules. In the Danish People's Party we would have liked to see a whole new way of taxing our cars, that is, a more technically based tax, where it is not, as it is today, about the value of the car, but where you look instead at what kind of fuel is used and how much the car pollutes or weighs or some other parameters. Because that would make it possible to have a neutral tax system which would probably be expected to settle on the positive side of electric cars because they typically pollute less and run on a very good fuel form etc. So in this way I think it would actually benefit many of the new technologies, where it is today value-based, which actually works against the new technologies, because they can be more expensive at first. So in the Danish People's Party we would have liked to have seen something else. I understand from the Danish Industry hearing that it is now the twelfth time that special rules are being made for electric cars, and I think it would be better to make a permanent solution in the form of a change in our car charges so that they became technology neutral. This brings me to the motion for a resolution from the SF, which we cannot support. Because it is very much a question of making special rules for a technology where we in the Danish People's Party believe that we should make a more general change, that is to say, technically based, and that there is therefore no need to make all these special arrangements. If I am to highlight a little funny in SF's proposal, it is that you want to set up a scheme so that private citizens can borrow and try an electric car for 1 month for 500 DKK. It may sound like a quite good arrangement if the industry itself made it, but I really do not think it is the task of the state to facilitate such a scheme. So I think it's a little funny. It will certainly cause some competitive differences out there, which I do not think are fair, by the state interfering and choosing a technology out. That is why I think it would be a much more clean cut to make a general shift in vehicle taxes based on some technical things, rather than having the value-based system that exists today. These were supposed to be the words of the Danish People's Party.

ParlaMint-DK_20170510162435	Thank you. First of all, I would like to thank the parties to the agreement and the parties to the consultation for a rapid legislative process. With agreement, we can reach a long way in a short time, and it is my hope that we can have the law packed and ready for 1 July. For goodness' sake, let me just list the most important elements of the bill, i.e. L 210: Up to 2019, or until 5,000 new electric cars are registered, the registration fee for electric cars, plugin hybrid cars and fuel cell cars is maintained at 20 per cent. of the general tax. Until 2021, we will grant a tax deduction of DKK 1,700. for every kilowatt hour in the electric car's battery. Until 2019 we maintain the low process rate for commercial charging of electric cars. Until 2024 we maintain the special rule of low electricity tax for electric buses. Finally, we are examining the possibility of a longer phase-in period for gas vehicles. We know the background to the bill: the sale of electric cars rose by rocket speed, as consumers were given the prospect of a normalisation of the tax or a gradual normalisation. When the first step came into force, the figure fell sharply, and in this way we learned two things: the electric cars were and are apparently not mature to compete under normal market conditions. Furthermore, consumers were not or are not mature to give priority at this stage to electricity rather than petrol, diesel or gas at the prices that exist. However, let us keep our targets in mind and keep our hands under the electric car market so that we still sell more electric cars towards a breakthrough for electric cars and can compete under normal market conditions. The Danes must buy electric cars because they want a better car, not because taxes have made all other cars more expensive. That must be the goal. The government has committed itself to the green transition, and we keep our promise. Denmark must remain a green frontrunner. But the green transition should not make it more expensive to be a Dane. It should not distort market forces, it should not lower the overall purchasing power of society, it should not lower the Danes' desire to go to work, nor should it empty the Treasury. With the bill we are discussing today, we are giving the electric cars a long and strong push. With the new agreement, it is our hope and intention that the electric cars will keep their balance in the future, and fortunately there are many indications of this. Electric cars are already very energy efficient. Today, 1 km in an electric car costs more than a third less in taxes than 1 km in a petrol car. In the hearings you can read that we will soon have a new generation of charging stations which will soon be able to load an electric car up to 10 minutes so that it can run 150 km, and in the slightly longer term it will be charging to 350 km in 10 minutes. It will take no more time to think about electricity than gas, and if that forecast lasts, electric cars will very soon be much more competitive. There will soon be no more excuses left for not buying an electric car if producers then also manage to produce it more efficiently. And that is why, I hope, there will be no need for artificial measures or exemptions from taxes in the future either. It should also be borne in mind that the government, together with the parties to the PSO agreement, has ensured that Denmark, with the full abolition of the PSO tax, will become the EU's third-cheapest electricity country, and this will also promote the sale of electric cars in the future. And the bill therefore already meets several of the intentions of the SF motion for a resolution, B 106, which we are also discussing today. The bill already favours company cars and electric buses. Many public institutions have already actively chosen electric cars for their employees. In the motion for a resolution, the SF would like the public to compete with existing private offers such as: sharing car schemes, DriveNow or GreenMobility, know that the government is now about to offer people that they can borrow a car. I agree that consumer restraint on the purchase of electric cars is probably due to uncertainty about the capacity of the product, especially in relation to the scope of longer stretches. However, I would reject the fact that it is the Folketing that must provide for a system whereby private citizens can try an electric car. I think we can get on with this development already, and I think there are private offers that can offer this. SF will finance their proposals by making it more expensive for Danes to buy a traditional car. They will thus make it more expensive to be a Dane. Moreover, the SF's proposals will also help to distort Denmark, as electric cars are not as widespread in rural areas as they are in large cities, and of course we cannot support that. To pick up, I can say that the Left supports the bill from the government, L 210, but we cannot support the motion for a resolution from the SF. Thank you for the floor.

When you made that crack, an average car drove 16 km on the liter, and then you said: then it is reasonable that we make the crack there because it is the average, and if you drive less on the liter than that, you have to pay a little more, and if you drive longer on the liter than that, you must have a small gain out of it, a small deduction. Then it was apparently thought that nothing would change, and that is why it was made as a fixed number, instead of being dynamic and perhaps changing every two years or whatever you decided to do. ParlaMint-DK_20170510180405	The debate is open and I give the floor to the Minister for Taxation first. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170510180428	Thank you for that, President. We are now debating a resolution from the Enhedslisten, which wants the government to present an analysis of a new tax on biomass for district heating. And I would like to start by stressing that it is not the government's policy to introduce new taxes. It hasn't been, it isn't, and it won't be. For the same reason, we have a tax freeze. We recognize that Enhedslisten doesn't, but the government does. We have that policy, among other things. Because we do not want it to be more expensive to be a Dane, either by increasing taxes or by choosing political solutions that are more expensive than they necessarily need to be. After all, there is massive pressure on the energy sector from the so-called green interests, and we therefore need, in my view and in the view of the government, independent professional analyses on which our policy decisions are based. If we do not, there is a risk that the green transition may turn green, but that it will be very, very expensive, and that we will not set up the tax and subsidy system in the right socio-economic way. Unfortunately, the current expansion of biomass is a very bad example of this. On the one hand, a broad majority in the Folketing has agreed to spend a great deal of money on encouraging the cogeneration sector to convert to biomass on the basis of some political arguments. On the other hand, when the changeover has actually taken place and the economic consequences for the Treasury become apparent to the astonishing public, there will be demands for tightening up. And it is a political zigzag course, which no one at all does not invest, whether it is municipalities or private or the state for that matter can be served with. Therefore, we in the government are also pleased that a broad majority of the Danish Parliament's parties in the Energy Agreement in 2012 agreed an in-depth analysis of how taxes and subsidies can most cost-effectively support the green transition from fossil energy to renewable energy. And it is to avoid the situation that we politically choose to grant subsidies and support a special choice of fuel or a special form of energy, where we come in a situation where we spend the taxpayers' money on supporting something that could perhaps be better done on market terms. One could come into a situation where, at least more green energy could be obtained more quickly, the market was driving development, instead of driving the market, through special Qualifying Rules or a special subsidy, to a place that is not socio-economically the best place to achieve what is politically desired. The final partial analysis, which looks at the overall structures of the energy system, is expected to be completed this summer. And those parties that are part of the PSO agreement and who took responsibility for abolishing the PSO tax, I would like to acknowledge that for once again, I also agreed to look at how the socio-economically inappropriate conversion to biomass is avoided and how the use of heat pumps is promoted. The latter is also in the government base. The Enhedslisten chose to be outside the PSO agreement and thus did not agree on the biomass analysis, which will be carried out this spring. So it is my clear expectation that, with the analyses already agreed, we will have a solid technical basis for future adjustment of taxes and subsidies in the energy sector. That is the goal and the government fully agrees that we should have a green, rich and prosperous country. This is best achieved if we use energy taxes and subsidies in a cost-effective way based on sound professional analyses, which we in the Danish Parliament agree on. You can discuss politically what the target should be, how green it should be, how fast you should get there and how persistent it should be, but we must surely be able to agree that we are doing it in the most cost-effective way in the economy. I therefore hope that all parties in the Folketing will be involved in future discussions in this area, so that we can create good, stable framework conditions for investment in green energy. In the Government's view, the necessary analyses have already been agreed in the previous political agreements in this area and, on that basis, the Government rejects the Enhedslisten's motion for a resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20170510183451	Denmark is in the process of transition to a renewable energy system, and in this context there is a fairly broad consensus that what is at the heart of such a system is an electricity-based energy system. It is, of course, because most of the renewable energy on which we have to base the future is electricity-based. It is electricity from solar cells, it is electricity from wind turbines, and there will be many other sources that work best in an electricity-based energy system. That is why it is absolutely essential that the investments that are taking place at the moment, because we are in the midst of this rapid development, should go in the right direction. Investment in energy systems is something with repayment horizons of at least 15 years and often more, which is why we need to stop misinvestment as much as possible and get the ground fertilised to make the right investments instead. There I am pleased that a great many rapporteurs have already mentioned that what is the way forward is the heat pumps. The heat pumps will work very well in an electricity-based energy system, and here we are also in the Alternative. What is less clever is that solid biomass in particular is now being used as a transitional technology away from fossil energy to renewable energy. It is quite important that we do not get too much investment in the direction of solid biomass. That is what we have already heard in today's debate for a number of reasons. ParlaMint-DK_20170510185559	This bill has been an exceptionally long time along the way. It is not, in fact, the Minister's fault, but our own collective guilt, because we have been incredibly long to get the negotiations in place, and when we have it, it is because this is so complex and so dilemmaous. The reason why the proposal has even come into the world is that in a very few years there have been too many killings of staff in the country's settlements. It actually culminated last summer, and that is why it is about a year ago that we really got to grips with this. You can say that if you look at the psychiatry in general, that is not just at the settlements, but at how we deal with citizens who are very vulnerable and who are mentally ill in our society, I do not think that I exaggerate if I say that we take scandalously little of them. People who are physically ill receive quite a different and much better treatment than people who are mentally ill in Denmark in relation to how our welfare society grabs. Today, in fact, the Danish Nursing Council has come up with a study that shows that one in six nurses in the psychiatry has rejected people simply because there was no room. I just think you should do the thought experiment, that you showed up in the hospital with your kid who had broken his leg, and then you're rejected by a nurse because unfortunately there's no room to take care of him. After all, most people would not put up with it, and it would quite quickly land on the cover of the Extra Magazine. Nevertheless, that is what one experiences every day in the whole country. As a psychiatric patient, there is a risk of being rejected simply because there is no room. There's such a huge gap and backlog on the psychiatry. When we had the power of government ourselves, we put a great deal of effort and, by the way, a great deal of economy into this area, and one might then wonder where the 2.2 billion are. kr., which everything else equal is a real, really large amount, actually fell off. Of course, some of the secret is that the number of patients in the psychiatry is going to explode in these years. It is not because we get more who have the heavy mental disorders like schizophrenia and the like, it is simply because there is a huge explosion in the number of those who have anxiety and depression and other things in the lighter part of the scale in relation to mental illness. In my opinion, this is one of the biggest social problems on which we have in no way found a proper debate, and on which we have found no way of putting social structures around, and we therefore have a welfare safety net that is far too large. So when we deal with both this bill, 206, but also the next one, 207, it is in an attempt to make some progress towards creating a safety net that can actually catch people. I do not think that we are in any way in the end, and even if we put all the effort into it and all 179 members of the Danish Parliament spent all their time correcting the psychiatry, it would continue to take decades before we could be able to deliver the standard we can deliver on physical diseases. I believe that this must be recognised, both as a politician and as a society, when we take this debate. This means that there are no easy solutions. There are no solutions where people can be guaranteed the treatment they actually deserve. There are no solutions where we can guarantee that staff will not be injured, there are no solutions where we can guarantee that there will be no killing again. That kind of solution doesn't exist when we're dealing with this area. We simply cannot, even if we turn on all the taps, do it in a way so that we can provide those guarantees. It is a hugely frustrating situation to stand in as a politician because you would actually like to solve things, especially when it is the case that we are dealing with some of the country's most vulnerable people on the one hand and that we are dealing with some employees who work a certain amount out of their pants every day to ensure that the vulnerable fellow citizens get a better everyday life on the other. Yes, this specific proposal is about what to do on the settlement itself. I think we have such an outstanding issue about what we are doing with the settlements at all. The Social Democratic Party has previously announced that we would like to have a debate on whether we were right about structural reform at the time when we had the whole area of settlements despecialized. We don't think they did. The despecialisation in this area has been too great, i.e. a very large part of the specialist knowledge that was available when the settlements were under the old counties has been lost. Residents live together across different diagnoses, age groups, etc. in settlements that have difficulty in dealing professionally with all the challenges that are present. So I think that, after we have dealt with these legislative proposals today, which are some important steps in the right direction, we have a task in going in here 10 years after the structural reform and looking at what has been good because there is certainly also something that has been good and what has been bad and how we can then correct what has not been good enough. This first of the two legislative proposals we are discussing today is about something that has hit my sense of justice quite fundamentally. It has been one of the things that has been very important for the social democracy to get through in the negotiations, because in the country's settlements it is clear that when you deal with mentally ill people, sometimes things happen because you are mentally ill, and that means that things happen between residents who do not necessarily happen in an ordinary home rise somewhere else in Denmark. And it is not, of course, because some people have a desire to be evil with one another; it is simply a question of the fact that, for some people, the disease is reflected in outward-looking behaviour or cross-border behaviour and does not understand rejection. Therefore, sexual abuse, but also physical violence between residents is very common in the country's settlements compared to all other settlements in Denmark, just as we have seen these examples that violence against staff is all the more common here than it is in many other jobs. What is then the point of this bill is to say that at least one should have the same opportunities in the country's settlements as one has in the housing sector, namely that if residents have been in squabbling with one another and there is one party that has been violent towards another party or is particularly harassing, it must of course be the case that one can protect the victim from having to live up and down his perpetrator or up and down the person who is carrying out particularly harassing behavior. That is why things become so dilemmaous, because the resident we are moving must remember is an incredibly vulnerable person who has not had any intention of harming some others. And that's what makes it different than if it was just a regular housing facility, where some people behaved stupidly. So here we are dealing with a resident who has not necessarily had any intention, but as part of his illness has been cross-border and outgoing in his behaviour. I believe that a very good balance has been struck in the way this bill has been done, and that is why, of course, the Social Democratic Party can support it.

ParlaMint-DK_20170510190637	No one else has asked for brief comments. We thank the rapporteur and go further in the rapporteur's line to Mrs Karina Adsbøl from the Danish People's Party, who was there.
ParlaMint-DK_20170510190646	Yeah, I'm right here. Thank you. After all, this bill follows from an agreement on an action plan to prevent violence on housing offers, which the then government, in other words the Left Government, together with the Social Democracy, the Danish People's Party, the Liberal Alliance, the Alternative, the Radical Left, the Socialist People's Party and the Conservative People's Party on 14 October 2016, and that is a partial agreement under the Rate Pool Agreement. The draft law provides for the possibility for the municipal council, in special cases, to propose to the State Administration to decide that a resident should be moved to a particular accommodation offer if he is a major danger to, or exhibits, a particularly threatening or particularly harassing behaviour towards other residents and to the staff. The aim of the draft law is first and foremost to prevent violence and threats and to increase the security of residents and employees on accommodation offers in order to ensure a good environment and to provide assistance and support as soon as possible under other provisions of the legislation. Unfortunately, we have witnessed attacks and the killing of staff working with very sick people. Since 2012, several employees in settlements and welfare homes have lost their lives while doing their job. Violence and threats are an ever-recurring problem in the country's settlements for mentally ill people and other vulnerable citizens. That is why a political agreement has now been drawn up to help prevent and remedy this with the creation of 150 new seats. In the Danish People's Party we also believe that this is about norms, that there are enough staff to provide security for the mentally ill. In addition, we also believe that it is a question of having the residents searched for the right accommodation offer in the first place. I myself have worked in the caring profession for many years and know that it means all the world that there are enough staff to prevent conflicts and unrest and to create security and proximity for those who are in difficulty. A busy and hectic everyday life affects the residents on a settlement offer. The target group for this bill, which deals with relocations, is residents on a housing offer with a mental illness which is at risk or poses threatening behavior or particularly harassing behaviour. Today, a municipality cannot, as a rule, decide that a resident should remove a section 108-boy offer without consent, even if the resident is at risk or exhibits particularly threatening or harassing behaviour. Therefore, this draft law will give the municipal council a legal basis for this, and specific conditions are laid down which define and limit the possibility of relocation, so that only the possibility of stopping to move in very specific cases is permitted. And I have just mentioned those cases, namely that people are a danger to others and are harassing behaviour. It may also seem indefensible for the safety of the other residents or staff not to arrange for a transfer where circumstances make it absolutely necessary in the individual case and it can be demonstrated that the new accommodation offer is better suited to the needs of the person concerned. Moving without consent is a major interference with the right of self-determination, which is why it has also been proposed that it be included in Chapter 24 of the Service Act on the use of force. The purpose of the proposed provision is to apply it only in exceptional and exceptional cases where it has not been possible to motivate the resident concerned to consent to the move and where social pedagogy methods and other efforts have attempted to prevent the dangerous, threatening or harassing behaviour of the residents. I must be honest and admit that this has been a very difficult process and it has not been an easy course of negotiations, also because there are several elements that need to be taken into account. The safety of staff and other residents must be taken into account. It must be weighted high and, at the same time, we must also bear in mind that it is sick people we are dealing with. That is why we in the Danish People's Party wanted to see harassment behavior deleted. Because when are people harassing? How much will it take to do something as drastic as forced relocation? This bill must not become a slippery path in which people are threatened with forced relocation because a municipality wants to close a settlement offer or has found a cheaper accommodation offer. Therefore, it is also an area that we in the Danish People's Party will keep a close eye on and follow very closely, and we will already ask the Minister to keep both § 71 supervision, the Social Committee and the Health Committee regularly informed about it here, so that we can get an overview of how to apply this paragraph in practice, also with random checks on the evidence that the criteria have been met and that all efforts have been tested before resorting to this paragraph. We look forward to the committee reading and will also ask a number of follow-up questions along the way.

ParlaMint-DK_20170510192058	The background to this bill here, as several have already mentioned, is the tragic incidents in psychiatric settlements where severely ill citizens have committed violence and abuse against staff or other citizens. And we have also seen the worst that can be experienced at all, namely that staff have been killed. Whether we are talking about doctors, caregivers, users or relatives, it is from a wide range of organisations that the violence we see is the tragic consequence of a downgrade in the whole psychiatric area; too little staff in the settlements, too little training, too few beds and too little staff in hospital psychiatry, too poor prevention and too little rehabilitation work. That is why violence is very much a political responsibility. It is our responsibility and the responsibility of the municipalities and regions to ensure a proper framework for psychiatry. Therefore, the most important thing we can do is to ensure stable and adequate funding and also close follow-up to the fact that psychiatry is not the area that is constantly going to the bottom of the pile when it comes to giving priority to health care. In relation to the current draft law, we are concerned about the first part of the draft law that we should have the opportunity to transfer one citizen from one place of residence to another. It is a far-reaching interference in the individual's right to self-determination, and the wording that harassing behaviour can be a justification also opens up a wide-ranging use of the provision. Conversely, I also recognise that there may be cases where it is necessary to move a citizen against the will of the citizen for the sake of other residents. I think there is too little emphasis on where the citizen should be moved to. It is absolutely crucial for me to see that there is a better situation in which the citizen is not being chased around from one residence to the next and the next again. Then there is the second part of the bill that the municipality can impose a condition on the citizen that one should give up one's place on a settlement offer in order to gain access to these new, special psychiatric beds, which we will be discussing shortly. But we cannot support that part of the bill. I've never heard of situations where you can make a claim to a citizen to give up your residence in order to be admitted to the hospital. This does not apply to citizens who live in nursing homes, it does not apply to young people who live in a youth home, it does not apply to those who are disabled people who live on accommodation, nor does it apply to psychiatric patients who are admitted to an ordinary psychiatric ward. I think it is quite, quite unreasonable that in this way one can be met with a demand that one should make oneself homeless if one wants to have access to these new rehabilitation special psychiatric beds. Nor does it make any sense in the context of the objective of rehabilitating it. Because as part of that work, it is also about getting home from time to time and thus trying to make your life work. I also don't understand how you imagine it should motivate a citizen to accept this special offer if you're going to give up your home in this way. Then some rapporteurs here say that it may be that you do not have to return to the same housing offer again, and that is true. However, it would be very simple to resolve, with a requirement that a home be secured at the time of admission somewhere else. The most honest interpretation we hear from the Socialist rapporteur is that it is about money and resources, and I have to say that I think it is quite unreasonable that the very weakest citizens we are dealing with here should be involved in financing these new, special psychiatric beds themselves. One thing is that it is done via the rate pool, as you know, going from their own income. Another thing is that they must also do so by making themselves homeless. I would urge the Danish Parliament to listen to the call made by nine organizations in this area to take that demand out of the legislation. I can at least promise that we will table amendments on that part. If it is not taken out, we cannot support the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20170510193432	Thank you. This proposal is designed to prevent violence in settlements. We have seen a series of incidents which have had terrible consequences for employees and which have made life insecure for both employees and residents. We have witnessed both assaults and killings, and we simply have to respond to that. We would like to help residents and employees to have a better everyday life, and we therefore want to make it possible to move residents who are deemed to be at risk to others. The aim is to be able to move residents to another settlement that is better able to handle the current situation. It should be pointed out that it is only possible to force the transfer of residents if all other options have been exhausted and there can be no question of moving residents who are simply loud, annoying or otherwise difficult. They are citizens suffering from a mental illness which causes their ability to function to be significantly and permanently reduced, and they must be able to report to the State Administration that they are seriously at risk and exhibit particularly threatening or particularly harassing behaviour. It is therefore only in extreme situations that moving residents without consent can become a reality, and it must be demonstrated that the second residence is better for the citizen. In order to achieve this bill, we have been through a very long process and it has been a good dialogue with all stakeholders. Based on the input of professionals involved in the daily work of this group of citizens, various sequences have been corrected, added and omitted in this bill. But we still have to acknowledge that not everyone is 100 percent satisfied, and it will probably be very difficult to solve this great challenge in a way, so everyone is one hundred percent in agreement. But I feel that we have listened and responded in the best possible way, and I really hope that this bill will provide a safer and better everyday life for employees and residents. To get Mrs Stine Brix a little ahead of her, I would like to say that it is not the case that the existing housing offer should, if it is estimated to be up to six months, ask how appropriate it is to have two public housing tax-paid. You could look at that if you want to spend the money on helping as many citizens as possible. It is not certain that, after a stay in this special psychiatry, the citizen will return to the same settlement. This will again depend on a concrete and individual assessment. There is, of course, a rule in the proposal that we are going to deal with here afterwards that a single action plan should be drawn up, which is intended precisely to make the best possible progress after a stay in this special department, so that you enter a specific and individual assessment of where it is best for the individual citizen to get to where the individual citizen will live afterwards. After all, no one is left homeless on that basis, precisely because you ensure that you have a coherent course and you help the citizens all the way through the process. The Liberal Alliance therefore supports this proposal.

As I see it, this bill is an extension of the moving rules and a proposal that can improve the everyday life of many people. It is a bill that should allow for the possibility of moving without consent when a citizen with a mental illness on a residence offer has an outgoing and threatening behavior that stands in the way of providing the necessary effort both to the citizen and to the other residents. It is not sustainable that a single resident with violent, threatening or particularly harassing behaviour may prevent the other residents from benefiting from the assistance and support they need or that he or she makes it insecure and potentially dangerous for the employees to do their job because he or she is unable to recognise his or her need for help. One of the things we have discussed in depth during the negotiations of the agreement is that the target group also includes citizens with particularly harassing behaviour. I would like to take this opportunity to stress once again that it will not be possible to move citizens without consent because they have an annoying behavior because you think they are annoying or because you do not really think they fit in. That is certainly not how it should be interpreted, and it is actually quite precisely how it gets fenced in. There must be a particularly harassing behaviour, which is systematic in, for example, to be repetitive, to be present in specific situations or to specific persons. So moving a resident without consent cannot simply be discussed because he or she behaves loudly or in a similar way. It must also be demonstrated that it is not possible to address the problem of social education in the current housing offer. With this proposal, we will give the municipalities an extra opportunity to act once all other options have been exhausted. Then there is the whole debate about whether you suddenly become homeless when you are admitted to one of these 150 places. I really think that a great many of the rapporteurs have had some quite excellent answers. I can fully confirm that this is something that we have discussed very much with each other and with those stakeholders who have a very special knowledge of this field. In addition, I would just like to say that it may well be part of a citizen's plan that you are on a settlement offer, going into one of these places, for example for a shorter period, and that you are then discharged to come back to the settlement offer. In the same way, it may be part of another plan that a citizen enters one of these special places and would like to achieve such a progression that the citizen will actually end up being able to be discharged into a residence offer that takes account of an improved life situation. I think we owe it to the debate here today. So thank you for the floor. I look forward to the further examination of the draft law and, of course, I am available to answer any questions which the committee may wish to clarify during the committee's proceedings.

ParlaMint-DK_20170510201815	We therefore thank the rapporteur, and Mrs Jane Heitmann from the Left. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170510201823	Thank you. Today, we are first examining a bill on the creation of 150 places in psychiatry for a special group of vulnerable patients. These are not ordinary beds, but places with a particularly rehabilitating aim for a relatively small group of patients who are particularly outgoing, who have a serious psychiatric diagnosis, maybe an abuse, and who have several discontinued courses of treatment behind them. It is time for us to speed up our efforts. There are people who go to work every day with the danger of being molested, and there are residents in the settlements who experience insecurity, just as there are people out there who really need help. And it is both sad and tragic that we have been able to read again and again in the daily press about violence and the killing of municipal settlements. These are matters such as: The killing of Center Lindegården in Roskilde and Forsorgshjem Saxenhøj in Sakskøbing, which has made it clear to all of us that action is needed, and it has also made it clear to all of us that we have lacked tailor-made offers for the small and special group of patients who are living with a serious psychiatric diagnosis, an abuse and a life that is unworthy in many ways in a society like ours. We must and must do better. For us in the Left, it is crucial that with the new offer, which is anchored in the regions, there will be good opportunities to make intensive and holistic treatment and rehabilitation efforts for those patients who cannot be accommodated in the current offer. This wish has also been at the heart of the part-agreement on the rate pool, of which this draft law is a coinage, and it has been a long journey to achieve this. We have had many and long constructive discussions in the rapporteur's circle, and I welcome the fact that the stakeholders and patient associations who meet the target group have also played an active part in the process. There have been many ethical dilemmas to deal with in relation to the use of force on the wards, the right to a place of residence and voluntary admission against forced admission, to name but a few. The special places in the psychiatric wards are created with inspiration from Department M at the Psychiatric Center Sct. Hans in Roskilde. When it is Department M that we want as a model, it is because patients with a serious mental illness and abuse are treated here from a holistic approach. We need to get all the way around the patient, holistic, because the issues are often complex, and with the bill here we are making completely new demands on cooperation between municipalities and regions. Because here is not just about treatment and so goodbye and thanks no, the goal is that the patient should be able to live a more calm and stable life. And in order to qualify for a stay in the special psychiatric wards, a visitation forum must be set up which lays down specific criteria for the recommendation. For example: the patient must have unpredictable behaviour and have special social problems. Against this background, it is also important to stress here that a multi-disciplinary approach is aimed not only at treating competences but also at social professionals. Social workers and social educators will be associated with the department and ensure that the patient gets the tailor-made help. It should be safe to go to work every day, and it should be safe to stay in a place of residence. Any abuse must be treated and the patient must learn mastering techniques so that it is possible for the individual, with support, to live a more common life, if that is the case. Therefore, it is also important that a stay in the special departments has a certain length. The starting point is 3 to 6 months and that it is voluntary, although there can be force on the wards based on the Psychiatry Act, and this is good for the legal certainty of the individual patient. For us in the Left, it is and has been crucial that you have a sharp look at and for the patient. That is why I am pleased that the draft law here follows a duty to re-evaluate patients on demand and at least every six months. This ensures that you do not just do as usual, but actually relate to the individual and the place of standing which is the current position of the patient. As I mentioned earlier, it should be safe to go to work and live in a settlement. The bill here cannot therefore stand alone. There is also a need for other efforts to be made and for coordination to take place. Effort teams and permanent doctors in the settlements are among the initiatives aimed both at ensuring safety and strengthening the health of residents of the settlements. The Left can support the bill as put forward.

ParlaMint-DK_20170510202305	Thank you, President. The reason for this bill is, after all, the tragic events that have occurred in psychiatric settlements where severely ill citizens have committed violence or abuse against staff or other citizens. As several of my colleagues have already mentioned, it has been a very long process to arrive at the legislative proposal that is on the table here today. In the autumn pool agreement last year, all parties other than us from Enhedslisten entered into an agreement to make five specialized socialpsychiatric departments targeted those citizens who have severe mental illness and who also have an abuse and are extremely outgoing at the same time. That bill was very far-reaching. As something crucially new, it opened up the possibility of imposing on citizens because they were dangerous, even if they were not psychotic, which has otherwise been a requirement for the use of coercion since 1933. The bill was then shattered from virtually every side, doctors, nurses, relatives, users. For example: writes the Psychiatry Foundation and Mind in the pile of consultation answers that we have left, that the bill was in breach of both the Human Rights Convention, the Disability Convention and the Constitution. At the same time, the bill was also a step in the totally wrong direction that we have in our efforts to reduce the use of coercion in psychiatry. That is why I understand that there were some parties here in the Folketing who regretted the agreement they had made. I can only wonder why we got so far in the first place and that it would take so long for us to stand here today and get this work started. Now, however, we have a new proposal in which it is not possible to impose on these new special psychiatric beds, and where there is therefore no slippage in when to use force. And it's a good thing we came here. As several of my colleagues have already mentioned, the model here is Sankthan's department M, where they are very good at both psychiatric treatment and abuse treatment. After all, it is a department that exists today and that is possible to operate within the current legislation. That is why, of course, one might think twice about why it is necessary to make a legislative proposal. What I can read out is that the new thing is that you make a visitation forum where the municipalities are sitting with, but also that you try to put the plan together around the patient, and that is of course some good things. Far down the road, it might be possible to do so today, but it is becoming so little more binding. We think it is a very good idea to spread the good work that is going on at St. M's department already. There is no doubt that there are citizens with severe mental illness who also have abuse problems and complex social problems that will benefit from a treatment offer that reaches all around the citizen. One might add that the overall view would probably be beneficial in the whole of psychiatry and not just for this particular group. That is why, of course, we can also support the legislative proposal that is here, which is closely linked to the coinage of the money that has been set aside. However, we have a number of questions for the bill. I would like to highlight what I can now achieve here. On the one hand, there is the question of how many seats we really get. I understand that there is no guarantee that it will be 150 seats. It can easily get smaller. Finally, I would also like to highlight the link with the draft law we have just discussed, namely 206 from the Minister for Social Affairs, in which it seems that there is also a link in the funding that the municipalities can make demands on the citizens that, in order to be able to accommodate these special sections of the bed, they have to give up their accommodation in a housing offer. We find this extremely problematic. In my opinion, it can also be a source of tension for the citizen to accept this offer at all. Finally, a slightly more general comment. In my view, there is no doubt that the main problem in psychiatry is that psychiatry is as heavily under-priority as it is at all. As I also said when we dealt with Bill 206, the problem is that there is far too little staff in the settlements, far too little staff in the hospital psychiatry, that there is too little competence and there is too little time for training, too little time for prevention, too little time for rehabilitation. That is the main problem for me. If we had more resources, we would have more beds, I think we would actually be able to solve the problems to a much greater extent. It is just a comment, in relation to what we shall continue to do in the future in the Folketing when it comes to psychiatry. It is very much about ensuring more lasting and more stable funding, and I do not think that we will achieve this when we continue to finance measures in psychiatry via the rate pool. That is also just the last comment I will make, namely that the money must come back from the pool. I find that extremely problematic. We know, of course, that the rate pool is funded by the people who have the least to live for, and that is why, in our opinion, this is not a fair way of finding the money.

ParlaMint-DK_20170510211758	Thank you to Mrs Stine Brix. There are no brief comments. Welcome to Mrs. May-Britt Kattrup of the Liberal Alliance.
ParlaMint-DK_20170510211805	Thank you. In December 2016, the Folketing adopted a legislative amendment which changed the monopolist status of chiropractors in manual treatment of the spine. This means that from 1 July 2017, manual manipulation of the spine may be performed by persons other than authorised healthcare professionals, including registered alternative practitioners. In other countries with which we usually compare, it has been quite normal that registered alternative practitioners have also had a legal basis for carrying out this treatment. In these countries, problems of patient safety have not been identified in this context, and there is nothing to suggest that it should be a problem in Denmark that we have brought the law to reality. With the amendment of the law, we assumed that citizens who are treated by a registered alternative should have the same compensation rights as patients who are treated by an authorised healthcare professional, i.e. as under the patient's replacement. However, it has turned out that this is not the right solution. The legislative amendment that we are dealing with today therefore proposes that registered alternative practitioners should take out compulsory liability insurance with treatment coverage if they want to perform manipulation treatments of the spine. This means that citizens treated by registered alternative practitioners will not be covered by the rules of the Patient Compensation Act, but by the general rules of Danish law, also called culpa. It is more difficult for users to obtain compensation under the general rules for compensation than it is for patients to obtain compensation under the patient compensation scheme, which has just been established to allow patients to seek compensation for lenient damages rules than other citizens have. As a rule, citizens who are users of alternative treatment are not considered as patients, but as customers, and registered alternative practitioners are not part of the Danish healthcare system. That's why you can't, like Mr. Simpson. Flemming Møller Mortensen does so, saying that these patients are worse off than other patients, because they are not patients at all in this context. Of course, citizens need to know this, and that is why it is proposed to set up a system whereby practitioners should inform customers that they are not covered by the patient's compensation, but by general rules on compensation. Then, as a citizen on an enlightened basis, you can choose whether to use one or the other option. If you use the authorised health care professional, you will have the opportunity to receive both a public contribution to the treatment and access to the patient's replacement if something goes wrong. Thus, even though joint manipulation is no longer a field of activity reserved for chiropractors, chiropractors still have a favourable status. The scheme must be followed up after 3 years. Therefore, the insurance company in which the registered alternative practitioner has taken out his insurance is obliged to report once a year any reported damage caused by the rabs, we now call them this time as part of their manipulation of the spine. The Liberal Alliance supports the proposal, which increases free elections without depriving citizens of their right to compensation in the event of mistreatment.

ParlaMint-DK_20170510212842	Thank you. And now we have come to the Minister, the acting Minister for Health. Welcome to the platform.
ParlaMint-DK_20170510212851	Thank you very much, President. I would also like to thank the rapporteur for his comments on the draft law. As several speakers have said, it is a second round, because it is precisely as a consequence of the law passed by the Danish Parliament in December that registered alternative practitioners are now given the opportunity, with a minimum of 250 hours of training in manual treatment, to have the right to perform manipulation treatment of the spine. The government and the parties to the agreements behind this agreement have found it right and necessary, as has already been mentioned, to do so for all parties except the Social Democratic Party. the registered alternative practitioners mentioned earlier to carry out this type of manipulation treatment, thus ensuring that the treatments were no longer reserved for doctors and chiropractors, which they are not in other countries, nor were they in Denmark before 1991, when the chiropractors became authorised health professionals. It is a requirement that any damage caused by the treatment is covered by liability insurance, and so it was with the December law that it was provided that citizens who suffer damage following manipulation of the spine of a registered alternative treatment should be treated in the same way as citizens who are treated by an authorised healthcare professional, for example. a chiropractor, so that, in assessing whether the citizen is entitled to compensation, the same principles would have to be applied to the assessment of claims for compensation for the patient. And it is, as several rapporteurs have said, in the situation that has now arisen that it has actually been shown that there was little evidence that an insurance company would be willing to offer insurance to registered alternative practitioners under conditions where the principles of the patient compensation scheme are applied without the damage being placed entirely under the patient compensation scheme itself. The parties to the political agreement have therefore agreed to abolish the requirement, as described in the December 2016 Act, that users of alternative treatment should be treated in the same way as patients who are injured in the public health system. It should be noted that the requirement for liability insurance covering damage caused by registered alternative practitioners is part of the political agreement, as is also apparent from the text of the agreement, but it is not clear from the agreement that users are to be treated in the same way as patients in the public system, nor does that requirement appear in the text of the law itself, but therefore only as a result of the legislative recommendations. Therefore, as several rapporteurs have mentioned and call it a period or a curl, it is at least for that reason that the Danish Parliament is today dealing first with a correction, but a bill that will ensure the legal comments to the law that were then, but it is not a change to the original political agreement that is still being implemented with this bill. So the government is now proposing, in agreement with the parties behind the agreement, that we abolish the requirement that users of alternative treatment should be treated in the same way as patients who are injured in health care if they suffer damage following manipulation of the spine. Instead, it is proposed that any damage caused should be assessed in accordance with Danish law's general rules on compensation, i.e. the culpa standard. This makes very good sense, as several rapporteurs have also said, because users of alternative treatment are not patients in the Danish healthcare system. They are self-paying clients of a private person whom they have chosen and where any damage to treatment must be covered in accordance with the rules of private law and is therefore also governed by private law. It is, of course, important that the user is aware of this difference, and that is also why, with this bill, we are proposing that registered alternative practitioners should sign that they are not covered by the public patient compensation scheme, but instead have drawn up private liability insurance covering any possible treatment damage, because then the client can make a voluntary choice on a clearly stated basis. And both therapists and insurance companies will be required to report any reported damage to the Danish Health Service, which will then annually determine the number. When, as agreed with the political parties behind the agreement, we meet again in the contract circle in three years' time, we have a concrete basis for comparison of the number of reported and recognised damages resulting from manipulation treatment, whether they have arisen from a public health professional or from a private practitioner. So, on behalf of the government, I welcome the fact that we now, it seems, are in line with this very broad political agreement, which allows the Danes to choose legally, on an enlightened basis, the manual treatment of their own body, as they wish, whether it be with a chiropractor, a physiotherapist or a registered alternative treater, who has a minimum of 250 hours of instruction in manual treatment and who, at the same time, meets the insurance requirements that we are proposing today with the bill. So, with these comments, I would like to say that I look forward to a good committee and that the Minister for Health will, of course, be ready to respond to any questions the committee may have on the way.

The agreement now before us runs until the end of August this year. What the bill is about is that we must ensure that there is economic security for the activities of general practice after 1 September 2017. What lies in the proposed legislative proposal is that we have to take a position on ensuring budgetary certainty for the time part, but there is nothing in the legislative proposal that deals with substantive changes. However, even though doctors now act as private operators, a large part of the benefits are paid by the public authorities. Therefore, this negotiating situation cannot be fully compared with other agreements in the private labour market. The money with which the regions pay the doctors is money paid by the citizens in taxes. That is why we have a duty to keep the costs under control. What governs the economy here is the so-called economic protocol that acts as an economic ceiling. Without that, there will be no control over the costs. The economic protocol expires on 31 August, while the agreement between the regions and doctors continues unless one of the parties terminates it at six months' notice. So even if you do not agree on a new agreement, the old one will continue until further notice. That is welcome, and I sincerely hope that the parties will agree to renew it. In the meantime, however, we need to control the expenditure ceiling and I therefore see no alternative but to extend the protocol. It is important to emphasise that there is no change in the content of the agreement, but that we merely extend the existing agreement in order to give the parties time to continue the negotiations in peace and quiet. Benefits and fees are set out in the agreement itself and are therefore not affected by the bill. I hope that in this way the PLO and the regions will have the time to agree on a good agreement without interference. The Liberal Alliance therefore supports the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20170510220238	The general practitioners have a very important role to play in the Danish health care system. Every day they take on a great responsibility in the treatment of their patients, and both good preventive action and treatment are based on trust and security between patient and doctor. Very many Danes attach great importance to and are very happy that they have their own doctor who knows both themselves and their children and family very well. One of the prerequisites for this is a long-term patient-doctor relationship, which creates a detailed knowledge of the family, which contributes to coherence, security and holistic treatment efforts. It is important that we in the Danish Parliament send out a signal that we support the general practice and the great efforts of the general practitioners every day, and that we want to help more people to train in general practitioners, as we did in the context of the broad political agreement we made on medical coverage. It is important that in future we have the general practitioners we need as we grow older and live longer. The aim of the draft law here is to extend the so-called General Practice Financial Protocol until an agreement on the renewal of the General Practice Agreement has entered into force. This ensures that there is still an economic framework for the sector after the end of the current financial protocol and that the Region's Pay and Tax Board and the Organization of Practising Doctors have time to negotiate an agreement. It is important for me to stress that this is not a legislative intervention in the negotiations. We're not interfering with the deal. The legislative proposal we are adopting does not have economic or administrative consequences for private practitioners, the state, the municipalities or the regions. This is merely a continuation of the economic framework in force in the 2014 Economic Protocol to the Agreement, which expires here on 31 August 2017 The Economic Protocol will lapse when an Agreement renewing the Agreement has entered into force or when the Agreement may be terminated after termination by one of the Parties to the Agreement, which may take place at a notice of 6 months. On the conservative side, we hope that an agreement can soon be reached, and it would seem that it is quite little that divides the parties. We can support an extension of the financial protocol so that the parties have time to negotiate an agreement. If they conclude an agreement so that it can enter into force before 1 September, there will be no need for the extension here, and then the law we are now adopting will lapse. Finally, I would also like to thank the working Minister for Health for his very good cooperation. I am fortunate that I also meet you in your role as Minister for Gender Equality, as I am also rapporteur in this area.
